    SHIP RECOGNITION PROCEDURE

   The basic principles of visual recognition
apply equally to aircraft and ship recognition,
end the techniques for imparting or acquiring
these skills are similar. Instant and positive
identification through perception of total form
is the ultimate goal in both cases. However,
since the distinguishing features between some
ship classes are very slight, considerable
analysis of armament disposition, and of

mast, stack, and superstructure arrangement

is often necessary before the overall pattern
assumes unmistakable individuality to the
observer. Less trained or experienced obser-
vers may be obliged to make such an analysis

U. S.—CVA FORRESTAL Class                     CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

CVA 61-CVA 62                                 The FORRESTAL Class is the largest
                                              class of aircraft cerriers ever to be built
CVA 5¢—FORRESTAL. 1954 (artist’s conception)  by any country. These carriers were

                                              originally planned as flush-deck cer-
                                              riers with a retractable island stricture,
                                              but this was changed and FORRESTAL
                                              was completed with a fixed island on
                                              the starboard side. These ships have
                                              incorporated the latest equipments of
                                              all types. For recognition purposes.
                                              the ships of this class can be easily

                                              identified by their size. From the air

                                              the one port and chree starboard deck-
                                              edge elevators, the extended flight deck

                                              forward of the island structure, the four

                                            “27C Conversion’’, is a modernized version of the

                                            similar in appearance to the ORISKANY Class.

                                            The modernization of the HANCOCK Class was

                                            more extensive, however, and included the in-
                                            stallation of steam catapults and a starboard

                                            deck edge elevator aft. The HANCOCK, CVA

                                            19, was the first unit to receive this moderniza-
                                            tion. Units of the HANCOCK Class are now
                                            undergoing a conversion which will include the

                                            addition of an angled flight deck and other

                                            changes. SHANGRI-LA, CVA 38, was first

                                             bridge structure, large crane forward

CA 45—WICHITA.   Very similar in appearance to the BROOK-
                 LYN Class light cruisers, the WICHITA

                 has often been unofficially called an 8” gun

                 BROOKLYN. She is the last U. S. heavy

                 cruiser built under the terms of the London
                 Naval Treaty of 1930, and introduced the

                 flush-deck, high transom stern and built-in

                 fan tail hangar, in American heavy cruis-
                 ers. In heavy cruiser design development

                 she marks a transitional step between the

                 NEW ORLEANS and BALTIMORE
                 Classes. Like the BROOKLYN’s, tne
                 WICHITA has the two closely spaced rak-
                 ing stacks and widely spaced pole masts.

                 She differs from the BROOKLYN’s mainly

                 in number and size of main battery turrets.
                 DISPL.—10,000 tons
                 LENGTH—608’; BEAM—62’ ;

                    DRAFT—25’
                 ARMAMENT—29 8”/55; 8 5”/38 DP
                 SPEED—32 knots; COMPLETED—1939

CL 144-WORCESTER. 1952                         This class represents a considerable depar-
                                               ture from the CLEVELAND and FARGG
                                               Class light cruisers. Substantially heavier
                                               than the CLEVELAND Class these units

                                               mount twelve 6” dual-purpose guns in a
                                               twin turret arrangement similar to the

                                               JUNEAU. Four turrets are on the main
                                               deck while numbers 3 and 4 are up a level.
                                               The WORCESTER in addition to size d:f-
                                               fers from the JUNEAU, SPOKANE and

                                               FRESNO in the closer spacing of the

                                               stacks, smaller bridge, mounting of cata-
                                               pults and the round stern. The omission

                                               of broadside 5” gun twin turrets is another

                                               salient recognition feature. Three-inch

                                               guns in twin mounts replace quad 40 mm

                                               gun batteries of earlier cruisers.

U.S.—DD FLETCHER Class                         CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

                                               The FLETCHER introduced a new

                                               basic destroyer design, and new stand-

                                               ards for size, sea-keeping ability, and
                                               sustained speed. Salient features of
                                               this design are: increased beam, rever-

                                               sion to the flush deck but with a con-

                                               ventional destroyer stern. FLET-
                                               CHER destroyers differ from the
                                               SUMNERs in main battery gun dis-
                                               position and in having a more narrow

                                               bridge. The FLETCHERs have prov-

                                               en themselves one of the most success-

                                               ful destroyer classes ever built for the

                                               Navy. They had an enviable war
                                               record in both the Atlantic and Pacifie
                                               theaters. Some units of this class still
                                               retain their original pole foremast.

SSG 282—TUNNY.   The SSG Class at the present time con-
                 sists of one ship, the TUNNY (SSG

                 282). TUNNY is a “Fleet’’ Type
                 submarine converted for the launch-
                 ing of guided missiles. This conver-
                 sion made her a ‘Fleet Snorkel’’ Type

                 submarine in bow and fairwater appear-
                 ance. For recognition purposes TUN-
                 NY has a pointed and raked bow, a
                 snorkel breathing apparatus, a stream-

                 lined fairwater, a large cylindrical tank

                 on the outer hull aft of the fairwater,

                 and a missile-launching rack aft of

                 this tank.

U.S.—SSR Class                                    CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

                                                The SSR Class is composed of submarines
                                                from ‘Fleet’? Types. These types have
                                                been converted te radar picket subma-
                                                rines (SSR). They carry the most ad-
                                                vanced electronic equipment, and are

                                               designed to perform scouting duties and

                                               serve as early warning picket submarines.
                                               These SSR submarines are similar in
                                               appearance to the ‘Fleet’? and ‘Fleet
                                               Snorkel’? Types, but differ from these
                                               types hy their relatively large radar
                                               antenna which is located on the top after
                                               section of the fairwater. Other antennas
                                               are mounted on the outer hull aft of the
                                               fairwater. Some SSR units, which are
                                               similar in fairwater erpearance to ‘Fleet
                                               Snorkel’”’ Types. were cut in two during

                                               their conversion to permit the installation

                                               of new electronic equipment. Two new
                                               hull sections were then added, thus in-
                                               creasing their length to about 343’ and
                                               their displacement to about 2,500 tons.

U.S.—APD CHARLES LAWRENCE Class

Converted from destroyer escort vessel

hulls, the APD is designed to carry

small units of combat troops and land-

ing craft (LCVP). Since APD’s are

small and fast, they are well suited for

such missions as transporting raiders

and underwster demolition teams.

Ships cf this class can be identified by

the stubby DE bow with single 5’’/38

DP gun house, a low bridge structure, a

long raised deck amidships, a trellis or             '

tripod mast aft nousing cargo booms,

and large boat davits amidships which

partially obscure the stack.

                                      recognized by a high freeboard with

                                      deck, which occupies two-thirds of the

                                      after part of the vessel, provides an
                                      easy means of identif:cation. The LSD

                                      28 Class is newer, larger, and faster
                                      than the LSD 1 Class. Ships of the

                                                     Note: LST 1156 class drawing includes
                                                     underwater hull.
                                                     The landing ship tank (LST) was de-
                                                     signed to allow the landing of tanks,
                                                     trucks, men, and their equipment di-
                                                     rectly on assault beaches, without
                                                     requiring the use of harbor facilities or
                                                     witnout off-loading from an anchorage.

                                                     In addition to fulfilling the duties for
                                                   _ which she was designed, the LST has

                                                     proved most versatile. Some have
                                                     been converted to perform a variety

                                                    of tasks such as flag ships, barracks

                                                    ships, repair ships, and personnel or
                                                    cargo carriers. Others have had a
                                                    flight deck installed and are used as
                                                    aircraft carriers for either piper cubs or
                                                    helicopters. The LST can be easily

                                                    recognized by the long bow with sheered

                                                    raised forecastle, and flat deck amid-
                                                    ships with superstructure grouped near

                                                    the stern. Either a pole or trellis mast

                                                    is located on this superstructure.

                                                    Note: Each LST is now named after a

                                                    county.

UK--CVA ARK ROYAL Class                                 CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

                                                  The ARK ROYAL Class consists of the
                                                  largest carriers ever built for the Royal
                                                  Navy. There are two units in this class,
                                                  EAGLE and ARK ROYAL. The ARK

                                                  ROYAL, expected to be completed for opera-

                                                  tional service in 1955, possesses the latest
                                                  equipmeiit for operating the newest jet air-
                                                  craft, including two steam catapults, deck-
                                                  edge elevator (side lift), and the angled

                                                  flight deck. The EAGLE, completed in 1941
                                                  and accepted for operational service by the
                                                  Royal Navy in 1952, is of 90% welded con-
                                                  struction. Similar in appearance to other
                                                  large British carriers, these carriers differ
                                                  mainly in overall size. Major appearance
                                                  differences within the class are the deck-

                                                  edge elevator and trellis foremast of the

                                                  ARK ROYAL. EAGLE has a tripod fore-
                                                  mast and no deck-edge elevator. Minor
                                                  differences can be noted in port side gun
                                                  arrangement and stern shape.

                                                  DISPL.— 36,800 tons

                                                  LENGTH—808’ (EAGLE 804’); BEAM -
                                                     113’; DRAFT—36’

             og  tage dee                      This battieship class plaved an important
                                               role in World War lI in the Atlantic, Medi-
KING GEORGE V. 1943                            terranean and the Pacific. The salient rec-

                                               ognition features are heavy block bridge

                                               and vertical stacks with a clear space be-
                                               tween them. From the air, the twin over

                                               quadruple turret forward and quadruple

                                               turret aft are strong identification fea-
                                               tures. The 14” guns have a greater effec-

                                               tive range than the 15” guns mounted in
                                               older battleships, since scrapped. The su-
                                               perstructure abaft the bridge, designed as

                                               a hangar, is now used as a movie theater.
                                               The catapult and aircraft equipment amid-

                                               ships has been removed in favor of in-

                                               creased accominodation and boat stowage.

                                               The 103’ beam is the narrowest of any

                                               modern 35,900-ton capital ship.

                                                                was expanded to match the old Japan-
    }
                                                                ese MOGAMI and United = States
    }
                                                                BROOKLYN Classes. These ships
ae     eee                                                      were the first British cruisers fitted
                                                                with triple 6’° gun turrets and with

                                                                aircraft hangars integrated with the
                                                                bridge structure. At first glance these

                                                                vessels have been mistaken for earlier

                                                                istic block-bridge structure with hangar

                                                BART, a cruiser of similar appearance in
                                                the Australian Navy. Note the step-up of

                                                the superstructure forward. Tripod masts
                                                are characteristic of British cruisers. Main
                                                battery is dual purpose.

MATAPAN. 1947                                                              Comparable to the U. 8S. FLETCHER
                                                                           and SUMNER Classes in dimensions
                                                                           and displacement, the ‘‘Battle’’ Class                 .
                                                                           destroyers appear to be a continuation       :
                                                                           of the LAFOREY and JAVELIN
                                                                           Classes. These units were designed as
                                                                           long-range antiaircraft fleet screening
                                                                           vessels, but they lack direct fire astern.
                                                                           The high freeboard and long forecastle
                                                                           make for good seagoing quaiities. The
                                                                           early ‘Battle’ Class destroyers were
                                                                           equipped with four 4.5’’ guns in twin

                                                                           mounts forward. Later units have a

                                                                           fifth 4.5’° gun mounted single abaft
                                                                           the stack. The unusually low and
                                                                           long bridge superstructure, as well as

                                                                           the absence of heavy guns aft, are

                                                                           recognition features. This class differs
                                                                           from tue “Emergency” and ‘“Inter-

                                                                           mediate’ Classes primarily in_ size,

                                                                           freeboard, and armament.

                                                                           DISPL.—2,315-2,400 tons

                                                                             DRAFT—17’ 6”

                                                        the exception of the gun mounts,

                                                        introduce the enclosed gun mounts on
                                                        British destroyers. Like most British

                                                        destroyers, these classes have a trellis
                                                        foremast, broken deck, and single stack.

                                                        squared-off stern destroyers. The after

                                                        house in both classes.

   UK—SS ‘‘A” Class             CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

                                British submarines can be identified
                                chiefly by the “gallows” or “goal
                                post” periscope shears and very nar-
                                row superstructure decks. The ‘A’”’
                                Class, however, differs slightly in that
                                they have three prominent periscopes.
                                Another distinctive feature of this
                                class is the sharp rise in the deck at
                                the bow. These units were reported
                                to have been designated for service in
                                the Pacific and have a different hull
                                shape than the “T’’ Class. Some
                                were painted a deep shade of sea-green,
                                but this was probably for experimental
                                purposes.

                                                                         During modernization the bridge was en-

USSR—BB CAVOUR Class  CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

CAVOUR Class.          Under the terms of the Italian Peace

CAVOUR Class.          Treaty and related agreements, the USSR

                       exchanged ARCHANGELSK (HMS
                       ROYAL SOVEREIGN) for GIULIO CE-

                       SARE. Oldest of Italian battleships to sur-

                       vive World War II, this unit was thor-

                       oughly mocernized during the late 1930’s.
                       Replacement of the original 13-12” gun
                       main battery, lighter guns and machinery,
                       and extensive reconstruction of hull, pro-
                       tective system and superstructure was un-

                       dertaken during this overhaul. Speed was

                       raised above the originally designed figure
                       ef 21 Knots. The 12.6” guns are reported

                       to be a new and powerful model and very
                       satisfactory. CESARE’S prominent tripod
                       mainmast distinguishes her from Italy’s

                       post-war fleet battleships of the DORIA

                       Class and cruisers of the GARIBALDI
                       Class.

                       DISPL.—23,622 tons

    1938-44

USSR—CA KRASNY KAVKAZ                        CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

KRASNY KAVKAZ. Note turrets and stern. 1934  Laid down under thé Tzarist Regime as a

                                             unit of the SVIETLANA Class of 5.1” gun

                                             fast scout cruisers, KRASNY KAVKAZ
                                             was considerably modified when her con-

                                             struction was resumed by the Soviets. She
                                             emerged 23 an under-gunned, diminutive
                                             “heavy cruiser” of greater technicai than
                                             military interest. The first cruiser to
                                             mount the 7.1” gun, the reason for her
                                             rearming may be surmised as a desire to

                                             start training and experimentation afloat
                                             with this Soviet-developed piece well in

                                             advance of the first commissioning of the

                                             KIROV Class units. Recognition features
                                             are: spacing and shapes of the stacks, fore-

                                             mast and trellis mainmast; and the Japa-

                                             nese style clipper bow. From the air the

                                             marked break in the forecastle deck just
                                             forward of No. 1 turret and the transom
                                             stern assist id“ntification,

                                             LENGTH—545’; BEAM—52’;

                                                 DRAFT—20’

USSR—CL CHAPAYEY Class .                        CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

The CHAPAYEYV Class cruisers were

laid down before World War iI, but

were not completed until 1950-51 due
to wartime delays. They are basically
four-turret versions of the KIROV

Class. Recognition characteristics in-

clude a broken deck line with the hull

break slightly forward of amidships,
four triple turrets (2 forward and 2 aft),
two upright or vertical capped stacks, a

heavy director on the control tower,
a poie foremast, a tripod mainmast

forward of the after stack, and a raked

square stern. Units of this class have
longer stacks and higher freeboard than
ships of the KIROV Class.

DISPL.—11,500 tons
LENGTH—656’; BEAM—69’;

   DRAFT—21’
ARMAMENT—12 6”; 8 3.9” AA;

   28 37 mm AA

SPEED—35 knots; COMPLETED —
   1950-51

                                                                         The KRASNY KRIM belongs to the

                                                                         last or SVIETLANA Class of light

                                                                         cruisers laid down and launched in

                                                                         Russia under the Imperial regime.
                                                                         Her design was well in advance of

                                                                         contemporary construction in other

                                                                         navies. A_ sister ship, CHERVO-
                                                                         NAYA UKRAINA, was sunk in shal-
                                                                         low water during World War II. The
                                                                         bridge—tripod mast—forward stack

                                                                         combination make this vessel readily
                                                                         distinguishable trom other _ three-

                                                                         stacked vessels. The casemate guns

                                                                         abreast the forward stack and main-
                                                                         mast belong to a bygone era.

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             power at the cost of their single 4.1” deck

“SHCH I’’ Class

                                                                                                                      R2LABSeec2

                                           This class is one of the most successful
                                           and popular Russian submarine designs
                                           evolved to date. The boats are well
                                           suited to operations along Russian
                                           maritime frontiers. Their cruising
                                           radius is somewhat restricted for ex-
                                           tended deep-sea patrols. Although
                                           the ,basic design is believed to be the

                                           same for all units, there are several]

                                           variations in superstructure arrunce-

                                                                     is a small coastal type with limited
     reeAeeatet
     pee nrwor                                                       radius of action. These submarines

                 ~               :  weactvonn~                       are known by numbers prefixed by
                 ~                                                   “M.’’ They can be easily shipped by
                                 Class.                              rail. ‘MALUTKA” means ‘‘baby”
                 “M-II”                                              or “‘little one.” Units of these classes
                                                                     were all built on a mass production

                                                                     basis and sent to their assembly ports
                                                                     in sections. There are five variations
                                                                     or classes, all characterized by their
                                                                     high freeboard and differing n:airly
                                                                     in conning tower appearance. The

                                                                     last variation, M-V, is believed to be

                                                                     somewhat larger and longer than the

                                                                     others.

                                                    This ship was loaned to the French
                                                    Navy by the British for five years with

                                                    the option of purchase in 1949 or 1951.

                                                    and then purchased from the British in

                                                    1951. She is used to provide training

                                                    in carrier flying and operation from
                                                    modern carriers. The ARROMAN-

                                                    CHES (ex-COLOSSUS) is identical
                                                    to the remainder of the British
                                                    MAJESTIC-COLOSSUS Class. Her
                                                    tall tripod mast on the superstructure
                                                    forward of the stack, short thin stack,

                                                    and low superstructure are recognition
                                                    characteristics.

                                                                        Named after a famous naval architect, the
                                                                        EMILE BERTIN introduced the first triple

                                                                        mounted turrets in the French Navy. On

                                                                        her trials the ship exceeded expectations,
                                                                        maintaining 37 knots for an hour in a 13-
                                                                        foot swell. Its maximum speed during
                                                                        trials was very close to 40 knots. At the
                                                                        present time it is said to be employed as a
                                                                        seagoing gunnery training ship. Like other
                                                                        French ships, it has exceptionally clean
                                                                        lines. The stacks are capped and widely

                                                                        separated, and like the LA GALISSON-
                                                                        NIERE, it has a very long and uncluttered
                                                                        quarter-deck. From the air the large AA

                                                                        sponson around the stern is a recognition
                                                                        feature.

FRANCE—DD LE FANTAS QUE Class CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

FANTASQUE. Note lower forward stack cap.  The destroyers in this ciass are sleek-look-
                                          ing, excellent sea boats. They .are excep-
TERRIBLE. Noie forward stack cap. 1943
                                          tionally fast; the LE TERRIBLE on trials

                                          reached a record speed of 45.25 knots with
                                          about 100,000 H.P. and an average for one
                                          hour being 44.9 knots with 94,200 H.P. On

                                          her eight-hour trials, she averaged 43
                                          knots. Units of this class bear some resem-
                                          blance to the cruiser EMILE BERTIN

                                          since both have wide stacks, widely sepa-
                                          rated. They differ recognitionally how-
                                          ever, in the absence of turrets on the LE

                                          FANTASQUE, and the presence of a tripod
                                          foremast and higher bridge superstructure
                                          on the EMILE BERTIN. There is no main-

                                          mast and the deck appears relatively clear.
                                          Triple torpedo tubes are located on each
                                          side amidships. The guns are not turreted
                                          but are singly mounted and shielded.

                                                       and MARCEAU are different. HOCHE

FRANCE—DD German ‘‘Type 34” Class     CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

This was the first series of regular fleet

destroyers built for the late German Navy

between World Wars. They introduced

quadruple torpedo tubes into German flo-

tillas. In service they proved less success-

ful than anticipated, especially in seakeep-

ing qualities and engineering maintenance.

Shield guns are disposed on the same plan

found in the U. S. FLETCHER Class. As

in all German World War II destroyers, the

main battery is not dual-purpose. The un-

equal stacks are the principal visible fea-

tures distinguishing these units from other

two-stackers. The Russian Navy also pos-

sesses “Type 34” Class units.

DISPL.—2,200 tons                                                                B

                                                                   Rated as a coast defense ship the GUSTAV

                                                                   V was reconstructed in 1929-30 when fun-
                                                                   nels were trunked into one and upper works
                                                                   considerably remodelled. In 1936—37 it was
                                                                   modernized and her armament modified.

                                                                   The large trunked vertical stack, heavy
                                                                   tripod foremast and absence of mainmast
                                                                   are all identifying features for this light

                                                                   battleship. Only two twin turrets are
                                                                   mounted.

                                                                   DISPL.—7,160 tons
                                                                   LENGTH—397’; BEAM—6\1’;

                                                                      DRAFT—23’

                                        ae

   SVERIGE. 1936                             Like the GUSTAV V, the DROTTNING
                                             VICTORIA and SVERIGE are rated as

                                             coastal defense ships. The appearance of

                                             the DROTTNING VICTORIA differs from

                                             the GUSTAV V mainly in the stacks, the
                                             former having two tall thin stacks with the
                                             forward one capped to prevent interference

                                             with bridge. The SVERIGE resembles the

                                             DROTTNING VICTORIA except for the
                                             backward bend of the forward stack, also

                                             to keep boiler fumes from the bridge.

                                             DISPL.—6,068—7,160 tons
                                             LENGTH—393-397’; BEAM—61’;

GA VLE. circa 1954                                                                         easily recognized by their new “bipod”

                                                                                           1941 but has since been saivaged and

                                tower bridge is considerably higher and
                                the mainmast is extremely heavy. The

                                reported to have given remarkably

ITALY—CL GARIBALDI Class                        CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

              The two units of this class are the
              ABRUZZI and the GARIBALDI.
              The drawing above depicts recent

              changes in bridge structure, super-
              structure and foremast. Like most
              other Italian ships, units of this class
              have upright capped stacks. Number
             one stack is partially obscured by the
             Superstructure. A new tripod fore-
             mast has been added. From the air
             the twin over triple turrets both fore
             and aft are recognition features. These
             ships represent the ultimate expansion

             of the original CONDOTTIERI design.

             DISPL.—9,802 tons

             LENGTH—614’: BEAM—61’:
                DRAFT—19’

                DP; 24 40 mm AA.

                                                                 mm AA mounts. Laid down in Ucto-
)                                          e                     ber 1931 and completed in July 1935,
                                                                 she made 39.4 knots on trials (not

                                                                 fully loaded). A sister ship, MUZIO

                                                                 ATTENDOLO, was sunk by Allied
                                                                 bombing at Naples in late 1942, and
                                                                 has recently been refloated. The verti-
                                                                 cal stacks are widely spaced and capped.
                                                                 Number two stack is located aft of the
                                                                 tripod mainmast. The bridge conning
                                                                 tower is a trunked cone. From the air
                                                                 the shape of tne deck near the bow and
                                                                 the absence of the number two turret

      ITALY—DD  SOLDATI                                           CONFIDENTIAL—Modified Handling Authorized

                         Class and GRECALE

                                               Recognitiona.ly these destroyers are

                                               similar to the Russian GORDIY
                                               Class but can be identified by the large
                                               ‘‘double”’ bridge structure and one gun-
                                              house forward and one aft. Another
                                              recognition feature is the lack of any
                                              sizable object uft of the trunked stack.
                                              This lack of superstructure coupled
                                              with the low freeboard gives these ships
                                              very little silhouette. Two near-sisters
                                              of the “Improved SOLPDATI” Class
                                              were transferred to the Russian Nevy

                                              in 1949.
